### What Joshua wants the student to experience

1. A teacher explaining the reasoning from the ground up. The first lesson explains what a condition and conditional statement are and why they matter on the LSAT. Ordinary meaning precedes the anchor example; the example precedes notation. Sufficient and necessary receive separate connected teaching beats before Group1/2 word families. Each new term and assessed operation must have been taught first, with enough explanation to work independently.
2. A natural lesson sequence like Intro to LR: explanation notes, meaningful worked visual, genuine prediction pause, interaction with specific feedback, guided reasoning, attached fresh practice and useful repair. The first four units form the present scope; later26 units are preserved. Teacher assigns scope and difficulty. Students see current assigned work, not a curriculum map, an assigned-start-stage narrative or a difficulty picker. The DEV preview's four raw-ID buttons are harness navigation and are excluded from production.
3. The writing standard Joshua chose. First READ THE COMPLETE `C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md`; assess actual lesson, feedback, flashcards, generated/reference notes and guided transitions against it. No synopsis substitution. Analyze wording, sentence-length variation, point of view, question purpose, cadence, transitions and repetition that teaches something new. Sound like a person reasoning beside the learner. Keep original intuitive examples; do not copy source jokes, claims, examples or mechanically force its routine. Avoid hype, stock catchphrases, em dashes, unexplained left/right instructions and vague praise.
4. Premium, memorable and visually engaging instruction. Quality means deliberate typography and hierarchy, readable measure/contrast, well-paced whitespace, varied compositions, drawings that expose reasoning, and controls that make consequences visible. An attractive hero alone is insufficient. Assess whether the body feels alive and clear, rather than repeated generic cards or a wall of text. Creative colors are authorized; matching Intro to LR's structure does not require its palette. Images, GIFs or video are useful only if they actually teach. Do not pretend incomplete Kling or NotebookLM media exist, or treat optional media as prerequisites. Judge the completed native interactive visual experience on its merits; identify specific opportunities if it falls short of his ambition.
5. Sound logic with no shortcuts. Guarantee versus tendency, rule versus case fact, enough versus the only way, required versus sufficient, the complete indicator target, limiting modifiers, clause-order variations, only versus the only, nonconditional inquiry/occasion uses, and must as obligation all matter. Conditional direction is not causation or time sequence. Scope ends at Group2; do not assess untaught contraposition, linking, Groups3/4 or quantity here. Original ready/packed illustrations allow alternatives without implying future labeling or established facts.
6. A usable platform. Keyboard alternatives, readable phone diagrams, pause/reduced motion, reset between examples/units, actual repair anchors, teacher scope gates and truthful saved progress. Never award mastery from a reveal or write a new edition marker merely because a build or navigation occurred. Preserve old drafts, attempts, exact historical question meanings and unrelated protected files.

## What must work

- Ground-up progression modeled on current Intro to LR: what conditionals are and why they matter, sufficient, necessary, then full Group 1 and Group 2 phases.
- Each group has actual sublessons: meaning, worked examples, indicator target, diagram, guided practice, independent practice, consolidation. Seven current indicators per group, complete modifiers and clause order included.
- Use Joshua's teaching voice, readable condition phrases, What Test. Preserve teacher assignments and current unit IDs. No student difficulty selector, assigned-stage or teaching-sequence jargon.
- Every visualization teaches a relation or limit: containment, fact-to-rule application, alternate permitted case, enough versus required, indicator-to-target alignment, if versus only if, only versus the only. Motion responds to learner actions and reduced motion. Keyboard equivalents for visual controls.
- Real answer-specific feedback and flashcards, with no fake mastery or claim to grade unassessed free-form logic.
- Responsive high-quality visual hierarchy, meaningful SVG illustrations, whitespace and varied composition. User explicitly overrides skill's exact color-theme matching requirement.
- Reuse adequate existing activities. Source-generated media are optional reinforcement, never prerequisites; show only completed reviewed assets. No placeholder media links or autoplay.
- Scope ends at Group 2. Do not silently teach Groups 3/4, quantity, chaining, or full contraposition. Sandra 2026-09-28 teaches a five-step method (identify indicator; label ideas; apply side rule; contrapositive; read both in English). Older notes use six steps. Explain diagramming within opening; preserve evolution in internal evidence, don't invent a canonical five-step method.

A native SVG infographic and flashcards can provide reviewed reinforcement if generated assets are unsuitable.

## Latest user corrections, 2026-10-02

Examples must be newly authored, intuitive and natural. Replace source-inspired dogs/mammals with our original order rule: Every order marked ready has been packed. Order A marked ready implies packed. Order B packed leaves ready status unknown; the interactive visual allows both placements and explicitly distinguishes a possibility from an established fact. Native SVG overview uses this same rule. Everyday Group 1/2 exercises were authored for this build rather than transcribed from competitor content.

Joshua switches video production to Kling. NotebookLM videos generated earlier remain internal reference only, never student-facing. Generated curriculum totals 296 questions; opening orientation and sufficient/necessary retain nine each. Review all newly added practice questions for correctness and scope.

12	# LSAT teaching: make the reasoning sound like someone thinking
13	
14	**This is Joshua's selected reference for writing LSAT teaching material.** Use it for bootcamp lessons, lecture notes, explanations, guided examples, drills, flashcards, quizzes, reference pages, and instructional feedback. It develops the teaching-content register in [the canonical teaching voice](../wiki/teaching-voice.md). That document still governs correspondence and marketing in their own registers.
15	
16	Joshua selected the supplied **Conditional Reasoning** transcription on 2026-10-02 because of its writing, tone, word choice, cadence, point of view, progression, and depth. The goal is to reproduce those qualities closely through original wording and examples. The central quality is patient, conversational reasoning that the reader can follow and eventually perform.
17	
18	The source is a calibration example. This guide separates **observed features** from **instructions for our material**. A source habit is not automatically a rule, a research finding, or permission to import a topic. Matching the voice does not require repeating the same metaphors, anecdotes, exercise sentences, or personal claims.
19

27	- [Questions that make the reader think](#questions-that-make-the-reader-think)
28	- [Transitions, repetition, metaphor, and emphasis](#transitions-explain-why-the-next-move-follows)
29	- [The complete source progression and depth](#the-complete-source-progression-and-depth)

35	## The evidence and coverage
36	
37	The [source summary](../wiki/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice.md) identifies the [preserved HTML](../raw/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice-standard/conditional_reasoning_transcription.html), [provenance](../raw/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice-standard/PROVENANCE.md), and SHA-256. Every supplied section, FAQ entry, table, worked diagram, and drill item was read. HTML line references below refer to that unchanged archived file.
38	
39	The document has six main sections and six subordinate headings. It ends with six basic drill items and an answer-key reference. One line is explicitly marked partially obscured. The answer key is not supplied. This analysis covers the entire attachment; it cannot reconstruct the missing line or establish the contents of the larger chapter.
40	
41	The HTML says its formatting was adjusted. We can examine what a pause, table, side note, or separated example does for learning. We cannot claim its font, spacing, color, or CSS exactly reproduces the original publication. No author is identified in the file. The exercise wording attributed to Shakespeare is separate from the narrator's teaching voice.
42	
43	## The voice in one original passage
44	
45	The following is a new calibration passage. Its example was created for this guide; it is not a source quotation or an approved bootcamp page.
46	
47	> A conditional tells us that if one thing is true, something else has to be true too.
48	>
49	> Suppose a shop has this rule: **Every parcel with a blue sticker has been inspected.**
50	>
51	> You pick up a parcel with a blue sticker. What do you know about it?
52	>
53	> It has been inspected. The rule covers every parcel with that sticker, including the one in your hand. A blue-sticker parcel that hadn't been inspected would break the rule.
54	>
55	> Now turn the parcel over. It has an inspection stamp, but you can't see a blue sticker. Does it have to have one?
56	>
57	> We don't know. The rule tells us something about parcels with blue stickers. It doesn't tell us that every inspected parcel gets a sticker.
58	>
59	> That's the distinction we need: **what the sentence makes certain, and what it leaves open.**
60	
61	Notice the opening definition before the example, the small prediction, the explanation connecting the rule to the case, and the change in what the reader is given. The last sentence names a distinction the reader has just seen. The passage has a conversational rhythm because it follows a person's thoughts. Casual vocabulary alone would not accomplish that.
62	
63	In an interactive lesson, pause after the first question and again after the inspection-stamp question. Reveal each explanation after the learner has had an opportunity to answer. The printed passage shows the whole progression for calibration.
64	
65	## How the teacher relates to the reader
66	
67	### The reader is beside the teacher
68	
69	The narrator talks directly to someone doing the work. “Let's,” “you,” and “we” keep the reader involved. The writing seldom steps back to describe what students generally do. It asks this reader to notice, try, decide, or check something now.
70	
71	The reader is treated as capable of understanding ordinary meaning. The teacher supplies support when a technical step is new. In the opening, the source first gives the literal relationship, then asks the reader to take its certainty seriously. Later, it encourages a guess before revealing the contrapositive. These are expectations of participation, not claims that everything should feel easy.
72	
73	**For our writing:** speak to the reader without making their intelligence the subject. Do not copy a line such as “Because you're a thinking human.” Give them a manageable question and the information to answer it. If they miss it, explain the precise move that went wrong.
74	
75	### Confidence lives in the reasoning
76	
77	The source is decisive about a valid consequence. It also says “We don't know” when the information cannot settle a question. These two kinds of confidence belong together. Clear teaching can be certain about the limits of what is known.
78	
79	The teacher does not apologize for asking the reader to think. The explanation makes the request reasonable. In the FAQ, the narrator acknowledges early mistakes and explains the value of practicing anyway. The tone combines patience with a specific demand: attempt the question, write the diagram, inspect the error.
80	
81	**For our writing:** state the consequence plainly and show why. Preserve legitimate uncertainty. Avoid changing a logical “could” into “must” to make the prose sound stronger. Avoid guarantees about learning outcomes, timing, or score changes unless we have evidence.
82	
83	### Warmth points at the work
84	
85	Encouragement is usually tied to an action. The reader can look back at the lesson, make a provisional attempt, learn from a wrong translation, or build confidence by checking work. The warmth comes from useful help and a teacher who expects progress.
86	
87	Useful form:
88	
89	> If the direction felt unclear, read the statement back in ordinary English. What does it say you need? Keep that complete idea in your diagram.
90	
91	Weak form:
92	
93	> You've got this! Conditionals are easy once you know the trick!
94	
95	The weak version offers reassurance without a way forward and minimizes the actual difficulty.
96	
97	### “I,” “we,” and “you” have different jobs
98	
99	| Pronoun | What it does in the source | Use in our material |
100	|---|---|---|
101	| **You** | Gives the reader an action or asks for a decision. | “Try the sentence in the other direction. Does it still say the same thing?” |
102	| **We / let's** | Makes the explanation a shared investigation. | “Let's keep the rule and change the fact.” |
103	| **I**, thinking aloud | Shows the teacher making a decision: noticing an indicator, identifying its target, placing a label. | “I see ‘requires.’ What does it say is required?” |
104	| **I**, hypothetical | Places the speaker inside a condition. | “If I'm taking this course, what does the sentence say I need?” |
105	| **I**, personal judgment | Gives a recommendation or reports experience. | Use only a genuine Joshua judgment or verified experience. Do not borrow the source narrator's student history. |
106	
107	Keep these roles stable. Do not rotate pronouns for cosmetic variety. In a platform instruction, “you” may be enough. In a teacher-authored walkthrough, “I” can make a decision visible. Marketing remains governed by its separate platform voice.
108	
109	## Sentence length and spoken cadence
110	
111	### Short sentences settle; longer sentences explain
112	
113	The source does not make every sentence short. A longer sentence introduces a situation, explains motivation, or qualifies advice. Short sentences supply a result, mark a limit, or redirect attention.
114	
115	The opening at HTML line 144 moves from a definition to a plain rephrasing and then the two-word landing “That's it.” Later, the source uses several brief sentences to think through a missing requirement, then states the consequence. The broad-purpose paragraph at line 152 is substantially longer. Its job is to explain why understanding the relationship matters across varied wording.
116	
117	**For our writing:** let each sentence carry a manageable thought. A difficult inference may need several sentences. Do not trim away the intermediate reason just to produce a punchy conclusion. A short landing belongs after the explanation has earned it.
118	
119	Original rhythm:
120	
121	> The inspection stamp tells us the parcel was inspected. It doesn't tell us which sticker the shop gave it afterward. The rule hasn't answered that question.
122	
123	The first sentence identifies what is known. The second identifies the missing connection. The third closes the uncertainty.
124	
125	### The observed numbers are calibration, not quotas
126	
127	An [approximate prose analysis](../raw/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice-standard/analysis-metrics.json) selects unclassified HTML paragraph elements, excluding the drill and obscured-line marker. It includes prose inside notes and FAQ answers, but excludes displayed examples, table cells, headings, and many short callouts. Words are alphanumeric tokens with internal apostrophes; sentences are split after `.`, `!`, or `?` followed by whitespace. This is a reproducible rough measure, not a linguistic parser.
128	
129	| Measure in that subset | Observation |
130	|---|---:|
131	| Prose paragraphs | 73 |
132	| Prose words | 2,743 |
133	| Approximate sentences | 223 |
134	| Median sentence length | 10 words |
135	| Mean sentence length | 12.3 words |
136	| Sentence range | 1 to 53 words |
137	| Sentences of 8 words or fewer | 76 |
138	| Sentences over 25 words | 15 |
139	| Median paragraph length | 33 words |
140	| Mean paragraph length | 37.6 words |
141	| Paragraph range | 4 to 112 words |
142	
143	There are 19 one-sentence and 16 two-sentence paragraphs in this subset. There are also paragraphs with seven, eight, and nine approximate sentences. This directly argues against making “three to five sentences per paragraph” a universal rule. The noticeable feature is variation matched to purpose, with space around a new task or example.
144	
145	Read the draft aloud. Listen for three failures: several dense ideas in one breath; a string of clipped statements that never connect; a recurring catchphrase that sounds inserted. Fix the reasoning and breath before counting words.
146	
147	### Contractions are normal; expansion creates contrast
148	
149	The source uses contractions throughout: I'm, you're, we've, we'll, there's, doesn't, don't, can't, couldn't, isn't, and what's. This includes serious explanation and procedure, not only playful asides.
150	
151	It sometimes expands a form for emphasis. “If I **am** breathing” makes the presence of the sufficient condition audible. We can use the same mechanism with new language:
152	
153	> You don't know whether the parcel has a sticker. You **do** know it was inspected.
154	
155	Do not mechanically contract every phrase. Keep a fuller form when it makes the distinction easier to hear.
156	
157	### Punctuation follows the voice
158	
159	Use full stops for changes in thought, colons before an example or sequence, and questions when a decision is genuinely at issue. Parentheses can hold a brief aside or notation clarification. Fragments are occasional conversational landings, not mandatory decorations.
160	
161	Joshua's no-em-dash rule governs our writing. The source's occasional stretched spelling, all caps, ellipses, or exclamations are observations, not a punctuation recipe. Repeated “Awesome!”, “Cool,” “That's it,” or “NO!” would exaggerate the surface while weakening the teaching.
162	
163	## Word choice and useful verbal habits
164	
165	### Ordinary language carries the formal idea
166	
167	The source retains sufficient condition, necessary condition, contrapositive, indicator, variable, and stimulus. It gives these words a familiar handle and shows what they mean. It does not hide the vocabulary the student will need.
168	
169	The conversational words are often functional: “have to,” “need,” “what happens,” “what do you know,” “the other side,” “what's left,” “this fits.” They describe a move the reader can make.
170	
171	| Word or phrase function | Source tendency | Writing instruction |
172	|---|---|---|
173	| Certainty | must, has to, necessary, no way around | Preserve the exact logical strength, then demonstrate it. |
174	| Possibility | might, could, may, we don't know | Say what remains open and why the rule leaves it open. |
175	| Drawing a consequence | so, that means, that implies, this tells me | Put the actual reason immediately before the consequence. |
176	| Giving a reason | because, since | Explain the matching relationship, not merely “because that's the rule.” |
177	| Inviting attention | let's look, notice, check, imagine | Name the thing to inspect or the fact to change. |
178	| Naming a boundary | but, that said, sometimes, unless | Keep the qualification beside the advice it limits. |
179	| Recovering context | so far, return to, now that | Remind the reader of the specific idea the new step depends on. |
180	| Testing an interpretation | this fits, does that follow, what would I need | Compare the proposed meaning with the whole original sentence. |
181	
182	Do not replace natural speech with “identify the guaranteed outcome,” “activate the sufficient trigger,” or “execute the conditional reasoning procedure” when the learner needs an explanation. Technical wording earns its place by helping them think.
183	
184	### Technical names arrive at the useful moment
185	
186	The source gives a simple relationship before dissecting sufficient and necessary. It explains the contrapositive in ordinary terms before presenting the flip-and-negate shortcut. It explains why symbols help before building the first diagram. The label arrives when there is a concrete idea to attach it to.
187	
188	This does not mean withholding a definition for suspense. Start with what the topic is. Then give the learner an experience that makes its vocabulary meaningful. A name should settle a distinction the reader has seen.
189	
190	### Small verbal irregularities create a person
191	
192	The source sometimes says “little squiggle,” “little bracket cage,” “for real,” or “a bunch of different ways.” It also occasionally uses elevated words such as “ballyhoo” or “cumbersome.” This combination makes the prose human without eliminating precision.
193	
194	For our material, choose the natural word for the situation. Do not manufacture slang or rare vocabulary to imitate personality. Do not import profanity, dismissive reactions, or author-specific tics. The recognizable quality should be a teacher's thoughtful judgment, not a checklist of eccentric phrases.
195	
196	## Questions that make the reader think
197	
198	Questions are one of the source's main ways of controlling pace. They have several distinct functions. Choose the function before writing the question.
199	
200	| Kind of question | What the source does | What our version should do |
201	|---|---|---|
202	| **Prediction before a reveal** | Coffee/walking thought experiment asks what the reader knows, gives a blank, and says STOP. | Ask a bounded question with enough information to answer. Wait for an attempt before showing the reasoning. |
203	| **One step inside a worked example** | Asks whether the sufficient condition could occur without the necessary burden. | Let the reader see the missing connection, then answer it in the walkthrough. |
204	| **Meaning check** | Personalized if/then asks what the speaker knows in a proposed situation. | Read the original sentence naturally and test whether the proposed diagram preserves it. |
205	| **Tempting reverse** | Tries the wrong sufficient condition and shows the interpretation failing. | Make the attractive wrong choice visible without ridiculing it. Explain the unsupported inference. |
206	| **Reader objection** | FAQ voices worry about time, confidence, and whether to write. | Use the learner's actual objection, then explain the decision criterion. |
207	| **Notation clarification** | Asks about parentheses and abbreviations. | Answer small questions where the notation first becomes relevant. |
208	| **Reason for practice** | Explains why the drill asks for written contrapositives. | Say what ability the task checks and why practice may be more explicit than test-day work. |
209	| **Boundary test** | Four-situation chart asks what has to happen in each case. | Compare the strongest justified answer with what could still be true. |
210	
211	An exposition question can receive an immediate answer. A student question intended to train reasoning needs an actual pause. Do not ask “What do you think?” and reveal the answer in the same breath. Do not add a rhetorical question to every paragraph merely to sound interactive.
212	
213	A useful check has three parts: a precise prompt, a learner decision, and a reasoned explanation. For example:
214	
215	> **Rule:** Every blue-sticker parcel has been inspected.
216	>
217	> **Fact:** This parcel has been inspected.
218	>
219	> Does the parcel have to have a blue sticker? Explain what the rule says about that direction.
220	
221	The feedback should identify the gap: the rule covers blue-sticker parcels; it hasn't said all inspected parcels have a blue sticker. “Incorrect. Review sufficient and necessary” is too thin.
222	
223	## Transitions explain why the next move follows
224	
225	The source's transitions keep the learner oriented. “So far” recovers what is established. “Let's return” reuses an anchor. “Now that we have a handle on” names the prerequisite for notation. “This leads me to the next question” connects a practical worry to its answer.
226	
227	A transition should answer one of these:
228	
229	1. What have we established that the next idea uses?
230	2. What is still unclear?
231	3. What will we change while holding the rule fixed?
232	4. Why is the next representation useful now?
233	
234	Original transitions:
235	
236	> We know what the sticker tells us. Now let's see what the inspection stamp tells us.
237	>
238	> These sentences use different words, but they describe the same relationship. Let's write that relationship once.
239	>
240	> The arrow is pointing the right way. Check one more thing: did we keep the whole condition?
241	
242	Do not fill the gap with a curriculum announcement such as “Next, we will explore necessary conditions.” Say why the requirement is the next thing worth examining.
243	
244	## Repetition changes the reader's view
245	
246	The breathing/air anchor appears in the opening, indicator families, contrapositive, fact chart, diagram, and target analysis. The reader keeps the same relationship while the representation changes. That saves the effort of understanding a new scenario at every turn.
247	
248	Within the contrapositive explanation, the source repeats the requirement in several forms: air is needed; breathing has to have air; without air the requirement is missing; the original condition cannot hold. Each repetition supplies a step or a new angle.
249	
250	For our writing, reuse an anchor while doing different work:
251	
252	1. Read the ordinary meaning.
253	2. Apply it to a case.
254	3. Identify the sufficient and necessary ideas.
255	4. Compare an unsupported reverse.
256	5. Reword the same relationship.
257	6. Show the diagram after its meaning is understood.
258	
259	Introduce a fresh example for independent practice. The learner should not be able to answer only because the lesson already solved that sentence.
260	
261	A repeated “Keep this” slogan without new reasoning is not explanatory repetition. Nor is rendering the same instructions twice in different panels. See the demonstrated corrections in [COPY-CORRECTIONS](../wiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md).
262	
263	## Metaphor gives the reader a usable handle
264	
265	The source uses doors, families, activation, burdens, brackets, and putting yourself in a condition's shoes. Each metaphor gives an abstract operation a physical or social handle. A small set of terms recurs, so the reader can keep using it.
266	
267	Transfer the method of choosing a metaphor, not the particular inventory. Prefer a familiar image that helps answer a logical question. Explain its literal meaning immediately. Remove it when it adds more things to remember than it clarifies.
268	
269	For instance, a ticket can illustrate a requirement only if the rule is stated carefully. “A ticket is required to enter” does not say a ticket guarantees entry. An analogy fails if it smuggles in that reverse.
270	
271	Do not build a competing metaphor for every sentence. Do not invent branded names for every skill. The standard technical terms remain the stable vocabulary.
272	
273	## Humor reduces tension without changing the rule
274	
275	The source's coffee obsession makes an unusual absolute rule memorable. Its reluctant-student aside acknowledges the experience of being asked to guess. The imagined green checkmark gives the reader's desire for certainty a recognizable shape.
276	
277	Humor can occur inside an explanation when it makes the thought easier to understand. Joshua's source selection supersedes the earlier blanket restriction that wit must stay outside the explanation. It does not authorize mockery, forced slang, exaggerated praise, or an expanding comic story.
278	
279	Use a light aside only when it helps the reader inhabit a situation or relax around a specific mistake. Keep it optional. A paragraph should still teach clearly if the aside is removed. Do not use the source's distinctive jokes as templates to be copied.
280	
281	Never turn first-person source anecdotes into Joshua's biography. “I ask every student this” requires that Joshua actually does. “Students always learn better this way” requires evidence or a narrower, truthful statement.
282	
283	## Emphasis and visuals identify the decision
284	
285	Bold in the source often marks the rule, an inference, a warning, or an instruction. Italics distinguish examples and sometimes emphasize a word that changes the contrast. A table lets the reader compare controlled variations. A STOP asks the reader to attempt something. A side note answers a local question without interrupting the main line for everyone.
286	
287	For our material:
288	
289	- Bold the relationship or phrase that controls the next decision. Do not bold most of a paragraph.
290	- Give an example its own space. The student should be able to find the sentence currently under discussion.
291	- Put comparisons side by side when the same rule or wording is being contrasted.
292	- Reveal diagram construction one meaningful step at a time. Explain each placement in English.
293	- Preserve a readable text equivalent for every visual. The illustration is an explanation, not a substitute for one.
294	- Make an interactive pause real. Let the learner choose when to reveal the reasoning.
295	- Give a side note a specific job: answer a question, define new notation, or qualify advice. Avoid a second full lesson in a callout.
296	
297	Joshua's notation correction still applies: teaching arrows should look like teaching notation, not little implementation-code boxes. Clear ordinary typography and meaningful labels should communicate the relationship.
298	
299	The source's adjusted HTML design is not a visual brand template. Our bootcamp should preserve the established Intro to LR student flow while using original visuals that serve the current concept. Student pages show the assigned work and the lesson itself; internal stage planning, assigned-start metadata, production notes, and selectable difficulty controls do not belong in the teaching prose.
300	
301	## The complete source progression and depth
302	
303	This map records what the supplied excerpt does in order. It is not an assertion that every future lesson should cover every listed topic.
304	
305	### 1. The relationship comes before its parts
306	
307	**The Art of the If/Then**, HTML lines 142 to 154, opens with a plain definition, a single familiar rule, a literal interpretation, several statements of certainty, and a reason to understand the relationship beneath varied language. The transition promises to take the same example apart.
308	
309	The depth comes from making “must” meaningful before presenting a list of words. The reader learns what kind of relationship they are looking for. The source also connects certainty to prior “Power Players” material, which is not included in this attachment. Our standalone introduction must supply any needed context itself.
310	
311	**Transfer:** first explain what a conditional says and why its direction matters. Then give a concrete example. Do not open with a diagramming task or make the definition a label attached after a quiz.
312	
313	### 2. Sufficient is explained through presence and absence
314	
315	**The Sufficient Condition**, lines 156 to 199, contains a definition, the recurring anchor, a metaphor, the possibility that the sufficient condition does not occur, a contrast between its absence and presence, and a clarification of “activation.” It then shows **eight indicator rows with eleven displayed example forms**, all expressing the same anchor relationship, and explains why language recognition cannot replace understanding.
316	
317	This is more than “sufficient goes left.” The text develops what the condition does, what it does not establish, and how wording changes around it.
318	
319	**Transfer:** give sufficient its own explanation, contrasting cases, and nearby practice. A left-side label is useful only after “enough for what?” has a clear answer.
320	
321	### 3. Necessary is developed as a requirement
322	
323	**The Necessary Condition**, lines 201 to 230, defines the other part, reuses the same rule, explains the requirement, and shows **ten indicator rows with eleven displayed forms**. It compares the varied wording with the sufficient-family statements and gives special attention to “only if.” It ends by explaining why symbols help isolate the relationship.
324	
325	The source uses the ordinary force of “necessary,” “required,” and “need” to make the technical term familiar. It does not jump directly to a memorized side rule.
326	
327	**Transfer:** explain what is required and why having a requirement is not the same as satisfying every condition for the result. Teach “if” and “only if” using a controlled comparison. Keep the target of each expression clear.
328	
329	### 4. The contrapositive is discovered before it is manipulated
330	
331	**The Contrapositive**, lines 234 to 285, first recovers the original relationship. It introduces a new coffee/walking thought experiment, removes the required condition, asks for a prediction, and explicitly pauses. The answer is derived from the unmet requirement. The term is then named, the original anchor revisited, and a four-situation chart sets limits on inference.
332	
333	The order is important: meaning, prediction, reason, name, comparison. The flip-and-negate shortcut appears later, after the reader has a reason to believe the result.
334	
335	**Transfer:** preserve this order when contraposition is actually in scope. Do not rush the full procedure into the opening definition merely because it appears early in this source.
336	
337	### 5. Notation is introduced as a solution to varied wording
338	
339	**How to Diagram Conditionals**, lines 289 to 388, recovers the indicator families, explains the arrow, gives a four-step construction procedure, and builds one diagram in stages. It then explains negation, displays four original/contrapositive sign patterns, gives the shortcut, and distinguishes writing a contrapositive from reading one when needed.
340	
341	The first diagram is narrated: notice the indicator, place its target, then complete the other side. Its final appearance is not used as evidence that the learner understands how to make it.
342	
343	**Transfer:** show each operation and its reason. Preserve Joshua's method and meaningful labels rather than copying the source's four-step count or abbreviation rule.
344	
345	### 6. Practical objections receive substantial answers
346	
347	**Quick Conditional Diagramming FAQ**, lines 390 to 433, has five questions:
348	
349	| Reader question | Depth of the answer | Reusable teaching value |
350	|---|---|---|
351	| When should I diagram? | Three paragraphs distinguish early training from later judgment, discuss difficult wording and chains, and address avoidance caused by uncertainty. | Give a criterion, a reason, and an honest account of the learning process. |
352	| How do I know the diagram is correct? | Three paragraphs connect confidence with checked work and studying specific errors. | Teach a verification habit instead of offering confidence as a substitute for one. |
353	| Does diagramming take too long? | Two paragraphs explain the proposed tradeoff and separate learning from speed. | Acknowledge the concern; explain why the slower practice step is useful now. |
354	| Should I always write the contrapositive? | Two paragraphs qualify the advice. A separate note explains why the drills still require it. | Distinguish training evidence from the eventual performance habit. |
355	| How should I abbreviate? | Gives a short example, a complete diagram, and advice about compact labels. | Make the notation choice purposeful and readable. Joshua's label guidance governs where it differs. |
356	
357	This is unusually important to the overall tone. The writer gives practical uncertainty enough room to be answered. It is not squeezed into one generic reassurance.
358	
359	### 7. A useful shortcut is shown, then limited
360	
361	**Figuring Out The Language**, lines 437 to 471, defines an indicator's target and brackets the target in familiar sentences. It first shows the common placement immediately after the indicator. Then it gives five examples in which the target comes before the indicator, followed by three paired wording variations showing that the same indicator can switch position.
362	
363	The sequence deliberately prevents an attractive mechanical generalization. The reader sees why adjacency often helps and why it cannot replace reading the sentence.
364	
365	**Transfer:** teach the indicator's complete target. Change clause order while holding meaning steady. Include one placement trap only after the usual case is understood. Mark the obscured final line as missing evidence.
366	
367	### 8. A translation is checked by trying both interpretations
368	
369	**Your Friend, the Personalized If/Then**, lines 475 to 508, defines the check, explains its purpose, gives four steps, establishes the ordinary meaning of a new sentence, and works through a correct guess. It then deliberately tries the reversed guess and explains why that meaning fails.
370	
371	This adds a verification strategy after notation. It also models the experience of being uncertain without abandoning the task. The teacher does not merely say “use your intuition”; the reader sees how to test an interpretation.
372	
373	**Transfer:** use the imagined situation as a meaning check. Preserve who or what the sentence concerns. Personalization is optional if pretending to be an object makes the wording less natural.
374	
375	### 9. Practice changes the language while keeping the skill
376	
377	**Basic Conditional Drill**, lines 512 to 563, gives supportive instructions and six themed items, with original and contrapositive diagrams displayed in the supplied HTML. The task is the same across varied sentence forms. The source says the adapted sentences come from Shakespeare and points to an answer key not included here.
378	
379	**Transfer:** original practice can have a memorable theme, but it should change only what the learner is ready to handle. Reuse the skill, not the source's exercise wording. Show answers after an attempt and explain why each diagram preserves the complete ideas. Do not treat the displayed annotations as an independently verified answer key.
380	
381	The six items do not cover every expression in the source's own indicator tables. In particular, they do not independently train only, only if, depends, or most other necessary expressions. Their displayed diagrams also lack step-by-step explanations. Our Group 1/2 practice needs broader expression coverage and reasoned feedback; the source's basic set alone would not establish that coverage.
382	
383	## What to carry into the Conditional Bootcamp
384	
385	The immediate authoring scope remains **what conditionals are, why they matter, sufficient, necessary, Group 1, and Group 2**. The detailed source is a quality reference. Its later topics do not automatically enter the opening.
386	
387	| Part of our opening | What the learner should understand | Natural teaching progression | Nearby practice |
388	|---|---|---|---|
389	| **What a conditional says** | A statement makes one condition enough to require another. It does not establish that either condition occurs. | Plain definition, purpose, one stated rule, one case, one tempting reverse. | Decide what the given rule and fact establish; explain the limit. |
390	| **Why it matters on the LSAT** | A conclusion can rely on the direction of a rule or on a missing connection. Reversing a relationship can support the wrong answer. | Keep the beginner example; show one supported conclusion and one unsupported inference. | Choose the justified conclusion and name the connection the other answer would require. |
391	| **Sufficient** | Enough to establish the necessary condition under this rule; not the only possible way unless stated. | Same anchor, “enough for what?”, present-case explanation, absence leaves the other condition open. | Find the complete sufficient idea and say what it establishes. |
392	| **Necessary** | Required for the sufficient condition; presence alone does not establish the sufficient condition. | Same anchor, identify what must be there, compare requirement with guarantee. | Identify the requirement; reject an unsupported reverse with a reason. |
393	| **Translation and diagramming** | The diagram preserves the whole relationship, including conditions and qualifications. | Ordinary meaning, indicator, complete ideas, placement, English readback. Introduce later operations when their prerequisites are taught. | Construct a diagram in meaningful steps and compare it with the whole sentence. |
394	| **Group 1** | Joshua's Group 1 expressions identify the sufficient idea; sentence order can vary. | Teach a simple expression first, reuse meaning across variants, then alter position and scope. | Lesson, guided translation, fresh independent items, explained feedback. |
395	| **Group 2** | Joshua's Group 2 expressions identify the necessary idea; target and force must be read in context. | Teach the requirement first, compare “if” with “only if,” then develop each expression's target and nuances. | Separate Group 2 practice, then a controlled Group 1/2 comparison when both are learned. |
396	
397	These are curriculum planning rows. Student-facing headings should teach the actual concept, such as **What does this sentence require?** They should not expose “Joshua's assigned starting stage,” internal sequencing metadata, or a difficulty selector. Joshua assigns the appropriate material; the learner works through that assigned lesson and its practice.
398	
399	### Give an idea several teachable moments
400	
401	The source rarely handles a significant concept with one definition and one answer. It may need a literal explanation, a familiar example, a variation, a pause, a mistaken interpretation, a correction, and a short practice task.
402	
403	For Group 1, that could become several small lessons: recognizing “if,” locating the complete if-clause, reading an if-clause at the end of the sentence, handling a universal expression, and checking that the diagram preserves the meaning. Group 2 deserves its own development. Do not flatten all expressions into a word table and assume coverage.
404	
405	Keep micro-assignments possible. A student who needs “only if” should be able to receive that work without repeating every beginner concept. A general assignment can connect the lessons into a full sequence. Beginner, medium, and advanced describe teacher-assigned work; optional timing belongs to medium and advanced practice after accuracy is established.
406	
407	### Preserve the real indicator groups
408	
409	The current reviewed opening materials use:
410	
411	- **Group 1:** if, when, where, all, the only, every, any.
412	- **Group 2:** only, only if, only when, only where, always, requires, must.
413	
414	See the [reviewed opening source constraints](../brainstorms/2026-09-30-conditional-curriculum/notebooklm-opening-assets/sufficient-necessary-infographic-prompt.txt). These lists are current project teaching groups, not a claim that every occurrence of each word mechanically diagrammed in isolation has the same function. The supplied source's two families have different members. Do not merge inventories without checking Joshua's notes and scope.
415	
416	“The only” especially requires its complete grammatical target. The only people admitted are members means **Admitted → Member**. Only members are admitted means the same relationship, reached through a different expression. The lesson must show the meaning rather than encourage a next-word shortcut.
417	
418	### Preserve Joshua's five steps
419	
420	In [Sandra's 2026-09-28 transcript](../students/sandra/transcript-downloads/2026-09-28-sandra-notes-by-gemini.md), at 00:43:22, Joshua gives this sequence: identify the indicator; label the two ideas; apply the left/right rule; find the contrapositive; say it back in English. This is a summary of his sequence, not a verbatim quotation. At 00:37:08, he stresses understanding and reading the diagram rather than retaining bare letters.
421	
422	The supplied source has four construction steps. Do not quietly replace Joshua's five with those four, or claim a shortened opening exercise has taught all five. In a gradual introduction, teach the operations whose prerequisites have been established and identify the full contrapositive step when that lesson is reached. Keep English readback visible as a meaning check throughout.
423	
424	### Carry these improvements forward
425	
426	1. **Define before demonstrating.** Start by explaining what a conditional says. Let the example make the definition concrete.
427	2. **Explain the purpose through a decision.** Show how direction changes the conclusion the learner may draw. Avoid unsupported prevalence statistics.
428	3. **Keep one relationship stable while changing the view.** Ordinary sentence, case fact, roles, wording variation, and diagram should fit together.
429	4. **Make each pause a useful thinking task.** Ask for a prediction or explanation, then reveal the reason.
430	5. **Show a mistake being detected.** A wrong arrow becomes understandable when its English meaning fails the original sentence.
431	6. **Teach the target before the placement shortcut becomes a habit.** Complete ideas and their qualifications matter.
432	7. **Explain why the drill exists.** The learner should know what ability they are practicing.
433	8. **Use fresh practice.** Worked examples teach the method; new items show whether the method transfers.
434	9. **Give practical objections an answer.** Time, confidence, notation, and when to write deserve specific guidance.
435	10. **Keep later content in its proper lesson.** Contraposition, chains, Groups 3/4, and quantities receive their own development; a quality reference does not override prerequisites.
436	
437	## Accuracy and method boundaries
438	
439	Writing that sounds like the source must still preserve correct logic and Joshua's chosen method.
440	
441	| Source simplification or context | Boundary for our material |
442	|---|---|
443	| “Ignore the conditional” when sufficient is absent or necessary is present. | For this one rule and fact, no new conclusion about the other condition follows. The rule remains available for later facts and chains. Never teach that it disappears. |
444	| Necessary has to happen “at some point.” | A conditional is not necessarily a time sequence or causal claim. Prefer “has to be true if the sufficient condition is true.” |
445	| The original and contrapositive are “all you know.” | The central warning is against an unsupported reverse or inverse. Equivalent formulations and other supplied rules still matter. State the immediate inference limits precisely. |
446	| Required words are described as obligations. | Preserve modality. “Interviewees must bring ID” can assert an obligation, not prove compliance. Diagram the stated requirement unless the facts establish compliance. |
447	| Indicator usually comes before target. | Read its full grammatical target. Targets can precede the indicator, and familiar words can serve other roles in context. |
448	| Shorten conditions to one or two letters, never more than four. | Joshua's clear condensed labels govern. Keep negation, scope, qualifiers, and readability. A short label that loses meaning is not an improvement. |
449	| Try a personalized sufficient guess. | Compare with the whole ordinary meaning and look for a counterexample. A feeling that an arrow is plausible is not enough. |
450	| Original and contrapositive training. | Explain why a drill requires explicit work and when test-day practice may differ. Do not turn a training requirement into a universal performance rule. |
451	| “Always” is in a necessary-family teaching list. | Establish the sentence's subject and relationship. It is not a universal instruction to place the next word on the right. |
452	| Confidence guarantees the diagram is correct. | Confidence can grow from practice, but correctness rests on preserving meaning and valid reasoning. Provide checks. |
453	| Timing and learning claims are absolute. | Treat them as the author's advice unless independently supported. Give a practical explanation without invented evidence. |
454	| References to Logic Games, a book, page 148, or named source tools. | Preserve the teaching function only where our course has an actual equivalent. Do not import obsolete or unavailable context. |
455	| A theme makes a drill memorable. | Keep each sentence logically interpretable and its answer reviewed. Novelty must not obscure the condition or require untaught knowledge. |
456	
457	For the parcel example, three possible cases distinguish the permitted relationship from an invalid reverse:
458	
459	- Blue sticker and inspected: fits the rule.
460	- Inspected without a blue sticker: also fits the rule.
461	- Blue sticker without inspection: breaks the rule.
462	
463	The rule does not itself say any such parcel exists. Examples of possible cases illustrate consistency, not existence claims. This distinction matters when moving from individual facts to general rules.
464	
465	## Calibration examples for different LSAT surfaces
466	
467	All examples below are original drafts for this reference. They demonstrate writing mechanisms; they are not assigned work or approved curriculum.
468	
469	### Explain sufficient and necessary without rushing
470	
471	> Every parcel with a blue sticker has been inspected.
472	>
473	> Suppose yours has a blue sticker. That's enough to know it has been inspected. You don't need another fact about the parcel. The rule has already told you what must be true of every parcel with that sticker.
474	>
475	> We call the blue sticker the **sufficient condition**. Sufficient means enough. Here, enough to establish inspection under this rule.
476	>
477	> Inspection is the **necessary condition**. A blue-sticker parcel has to meet that requirement. Otherwise it would be an exception to a rule that allows no exceptions.
478	>
479	> Now suppose a parcel has been inspected. Does it have to have a blue sticker?
480	>
481	> We still don't know. Inspection was required for the blue-sticker parcels. The shop never said those were the only parcels it inspected.
482	
483	This returns to meaning at every term. The example does not assert why the shop uses stickers or when they are applied. Those extra facts are unnecessary.
484	
485	### Teach a necessary expression through its whole meaning
486	
487	> Editing this newsletter requires an account.
488	>
489	> What does the sentence say the editor needs? An account. Keep that complete idea on the required side of the relationship:
490	>
491	> **Edits this newsletter → Has an account**
492	>
493	> Read it back: if someone edits this newsletter, they have an account. That matches the sentence.
494	>
495	> Try the reverse. If someone has an account, do they necessarily edit the newsletter? The sentence hasn't told us that. An account could belong to someone who only reads it.
496	
497	The final possible counterexample is introduced as something consistent with the sentence, not an asserted fact about this system. Technical placement has a semantic reason.
498	
499	### Give a flashcard a reasoning task
500	
501	**Front**
502	
503	> Every package in Locker 4 has been scanned. A package has been scanned. Must it be in Locker 4? Why?
504	
505	**Back**
506	
507	> No. The rule tells us about packages already in Locker 4. It doesn't say every scanned package goes there.
508	
509	This is more useful than a card that only asks “Necessary condition = which side?” Keep simple recall cards when recall is the actual skill; do not mistake that for complete reasoning practice.
510	
511	### Make feedback identify the missed connection
512	
513	> You used the sentence in the reverse direction. “Only registered visitors enter the archive” requires registration for entry. It hasn't said every registered visitor enters. Read the diagram as **Enters → Registered** and compare it with the sentence.
514	
515	The feedback names the mistake, shows the relevant meaning, and gives a repair. It avoids generic praise, mockery, and labels such as “weak reasoning.”
516	
517	### Explain an ordinary learning objection
518	
519	> “I understood the sentence. Why write it down?”
520	>
521	> With one short rule, you may not need to. When a passage gives you several relationships, a diagram lets you compare them without rereading the wording each time. In this exercise, write the relationship so we can check that you kept its direction and complete ideas.
522	
523	The answer gives a decision criterion and distinguishes the purpose of this exercise from a blanket rule about every test question.
524	
525	### Transfer the voice to another LSAT topic
526	
527	> A conclusion is the claim the author is asking you to accept. The other statements are supposed to support it.
528	>
529	> Nora says, “The library should stay open later because every evening seat was taken this week.” What is she trying to persuade us of?
530	>
531	> That the library should stay open later. The full seats are her reason. She expects that reason to support the proposed change.
532	>
533	> You don't have to agree with her. First, separate what she wants you to accept from what she gives you in support.
534	
535	The same mechanism works outside conditionals: definition, small example, question, explanation, and a useful boundary. No conditional topic has been imported into the conclusion lesson.
536	
537	## Revise the draft in a useful order
538	
539	### First, check the reasoning and scope
540	
541	List what is given, what follows, and why. Try to construct a case that satisfies the given information but makes the proposed conclusion false. If that case is possible, revise the logic before polishing the voice. Check modality, negation, quantifier scope, and whether the learner has actually been taught every required operation.
542	
543	For a rewrite, preserve Joshua's established method and explicit lesson scope. The new reference governs delivery. It does not authorize removing content that students need or adding source topics the current assignment does not cover.
544	
545	### Then, check the learner's experience
546	
547	Read as someone who has never seen the notation. Can they tell what the concept is? Can they answer the first question with what the page has taught? Does each new term refer to an idea they have seen? Does the page tell them why the correct answer follows?
548	
549	Put the explanation beside the point where it is needed. Do not rely on an interaction to imply a distinction the prose never explains. A student who cannot use the animation must still be able to learn from the text.
550	
551	### Then, listen to the voice
552	
553	Read aloud at an ordinary teaching pace. Replace stiff phrasing with the words the teacher would naturally use. Keep the intermediate thoughts. Vary sentence length where the task changes. Remove repeated catchphrases, inflated enthusiasm, branded metaphors, and irrelevant asides.
554	
555	Do not repair generic prose by scattering “Cool,” “Let's,” or “That's it” through it. Repair the missing reasoning, the abrupt transition, or the abstract example.
556	
557	### Finally, verify the student surface
558	
559	Check the actual prompt, answer state, labels, buttons, diagrams, and explanations. A good note can be undermined by a shell that exposes internal planning or reveals the answer too soon. Preview desktop and mobile when the interface changes. Keep the instructor's planning map separate from what the learner sees.
560	
561	## Use this rubric before handing over LSAT writing
562	
563	Score each dimension from 0 to 2: **0** absent or misleading; **1** partly present but needing revision; **2** clear and usable. This is an editorial tool, not a validated learning measure. A logical error or scope violation requires revision regardless of the total.
564	
565	| Dimension | What a strong draft does |
566	|---|---|
567	| **Ordinary meaning** | Defines the idea in language the reader can understand before notation. |
568	| **Concrete anchor** | Uses an original, small, unambiguous situation and states needed assumptions. |
569	| **Visible reasoning** | Shows the intermediate connection instead of asserting a result. |
570	| **Point of view** | Keeps the reader involved; uses teacher narration purposefully. |
571	| **Cadence** | Gives complex thoughts room and short conclusions a clear place to land. |
572	| **Vocabulary** | Keeps useful technical terms, explains them, and avoids internal jargon. |
573	| **Questions and pauses** | Asks a genuine decision and gives an appropriate chance to answer. |
574	| **Boundaries** | Shows what the information leaves open and why a tempting move fails. |
575	| **Progression** | Each operation has a prerequisite, a reason to appear, and adequate practice. |
576	| **Transfer** | Fresh questions require the taught skill, with reasoned feedback. |
577	| **Tone and warmth** | Expects effort, supports the next action, and never mocks uncertainty. |
578	| **Presentation** | Examples, comparisons, notation, and interactions clarify the reasoning. |
579	
580	Do not treat a high total as proof of an identical voice. Use the original source and Joshua's response to calibrate the subjective feel. His concrete corrections override a score.
581	
582	## A reusable writing brief
583	
584	Use this when giving another writer or agent the task. Supply the actual source, lesson scope, prerequisite knowledge, and approved method along with it.
585	
586	```text
587	Write this LSAT material in Joshua's selected teaching voice.
588	Read wiki/teaching-voice.md, references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md,
589	and wiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md before drafting.
590	
591	Keep the reader beside the teacher. Start with the ordinary meaning of
592	the idea, then make it concrete with an original, intuitive example.
593	Show each small inference in natural language. Ask the question a learner
594	would actually need to answer here. When the question is practice, leave
595	room for an attempt before revealing the reasoning.
596	
597	Vary sentence length by purpose. Let explanations breathe and use short
598	sentences to settle a consequence or a limit. Use contractions naturally.
599	Teach the technical term when there is a clear idea to attach it to.
600	Repeat an anchor only when the repetition changes the learner's view.
601	Show a tempting mistake and how to detect it when that distinction matters.
602	
603	Preserve Joshua's method, the exact logical strength, complete conditions,
604	and the selected topic scope. Give fresh practice with explained answers.
605	Do not copy the reference's examples, jokes, personal claims, or procedural
606	counts. No em dashes, hype, invented evidence, internal stage language,
607	or decorative catchphrases. Light humor is optional and must help the lesson.
608	
609	Read the result aloud, check every inference, and review the student-facing
610	prompt and answer states. If the reader cannot hear why the next thought
611	follows, revise the explanation before polishing the surface.
612	```
613	
614	## Keep this reference current
615	
616	This file is the detailed method reference, not a second canonical brand voice. [Teaching voice](../wiki/teaching-voice.md) remains the router. [COPY-CORRECTIONS](../wiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md) remains the record of demonstrated wording failures. Add Joshua's later calibration to the relevant section here rather than creating competing LSAT voice documents.
617	
618	The project-owned `lsat-u-voice` skill reads this standard. Recap and bootcamp workflows also read it explicitly. An older vendor voice skill describes a different writer blend; it must not override this Joshua-selected standard in this project. Managed plugin caches are preserved rather than silently rewritten.
619	
620	The source archive is immutable. Future evidence or revised attachments should be recorded as a separate generation with provenance, not written over the original.

60	export function orderedConditionalIdeas(item: Pick<Translation, 'sentence' | 'target' | 'other'>): [string, string] {
61	  let hash = 2166136261;
62	  for (const character of item.sentence) hash = Math.imul(hash ^ character.charCodeAt(0), 16777619);
63	  const reverseOrder = ((hash ^ (hash >>> 16)) >>> 0) % 2 === 1;
64	  return reverseOrder ? [item.other, item.target] : [item.target, item.other];
65	}

409	function TargetSentence({ item, marked }: { item: MappedTranslation; marked: boolean }) {
410	  const figure = useRef<HTMLElement>(null);
411	  const sentence = useRef<HTMLQuoteElement>(null);
412	  const indicator = useRef<HTMLElement>(null);
413	  const target = useRef<HTMLElement>(null);
414	  const [connector, setConnector] = useState<string | null>(null);
415	  const indicatorStart = item.sentence.toLowerCase().indexOf(item.word.toLowerCase());
416	  const targetStart = item.sentence.indexOf(item.sentenceTarget);
417	  if (indicatorStart < 0 || targetStart < 0) throw new Error(`Missing authored sentence span: ${item.sentence}`);
418	  const spans = [
419	    { start: indicatorStart, end: indicatorStart + item.word.length, kind: 'indicator' },
420	    { start: targetStart, end: targetStart + item.sentenceTarget.length, kind: 'target' },
421	  ].sort((a, b) => a.start - b.start);
422	  if (spans[0].end > spans[1].start) throw new Error(`Overlapping authored sentence spans: ${item.sentence}`);
423	
424	  useLayoutEffect(() => {
425	    if (!marked) { setConnector(null); return; }
426	    const update = () => {
427	      if (!figure.current || !sentence.current || !indicator.current || !target.current) return;
428	      const origin = figure.current.getBoundingClientRect();
429	      const firstRects = indicator.current.getClientRects();
430	      const targetRects = target.current.getClientRects();
431	      const first = firstRects[firstRects.length - 1];
432	      const last = targetRects[targetRects.length - 1];
433	      if (!first?.width || !last?.width) return;
434	      const x1 = (first.left + first.right) / 2 - origin.left;
435	      const x2 = (last.left + last.right) / 2 - origin.left;
436	      // Project both marked spans into a dedicated rail below the whole sentence.
437	      // Starting under an early line could otherwise cross a later wrapped clause.
438	      const railTop = sentence.current.getBoundingClientRect().bottom - origin.top + 10;
439	      const baseline = railTop + 28;
440	      setConnector(`M ${x1} ${railTop} V ${baseline} H ${x2} V ${railTop}`);
441	    };
442	    update();
443	    const observer = typeof ResizeObserver === 'undefined' ? null : new ResizeObserver(update);
444	    if (figure.current) observer?.observe(figure.current);
445	    window.addEventListener('resize', update);
446	    return () => { observer?.disconnect(); window.removeEventListener('resize', update); };
447	  }, [item.sentence, marked]);
448	
449	  if (!marked) return <blockquote>{item.sentence}</blockquote>;
450	  return <figure className="co-target-sentence" ref={figure}>
451	    <blockquote ref={sentence}>{item.sentence.slice(0, spans[0].start)}
452	      <mark className={`co-sentence-${spans[0].kind}`} ref={spans[0].kind === 'indicator' ? indicator : target}>{item.sentence.slice(spans[0].start, spans[0].end)}</mark>
453	      {item.sentence.slice(spans[0].end, spans[1].start)}
454	      <mark className={`co-sentence-${spans[1].kind}`} ref={spans[1].kind === 'indicator' ? indicator : target}>{item.sentence.slice(spans[1].start, spans[1].end)}</mark>
455	      {item.sentence.slice(spans[1].end)}
456	    </blockquote>
457	    {connector && <svg className="co-target-connector" aria-hidden="true"><path d={connector}/></svg>}
458	    <figcaption><span>Expression: <strong>{item.word}</strong></span><span>Complete target: <strong>{item.sentenceTarget}</strong></span></figcaption>
459	  </figure>;
460	}
461	
462	function TranslationLab({ id }: { id: string }) {
463	  const [index, setIndex] = useState(0);
464	  const [choice, setChoice] = useState<string | null>(null);
465	  const [diagram, setDiagram] = useState(false);
466	  const item = translationExamples[id][index];
467	  return <div className="co-lab"><p className="co-kicker">Read the whole idea</p><div className="co-word-tabs" role="group" aria-label="Indicator examples">{translationExamples[id].map((e, i) => <button key={e.word} aria-pressed={i === index} onClick={() => { setIndex(i); setChoice(null); setDiagram(false); }}>{e.word}</button>)}</div><TargetSentence item={item} marked={choice === item.target}/><p>What is <strong>{item.word}</strong> referring to?</p><div className="co-choices">{orderedConditionalIdeas(item).map(value => <button key={value} aria-pressed={choice === value} onClick={() => { setChoice(value); setDiagram(false); }}>{value}</button>)}</div><div role="status">{choice && <div className="co-feedback"><strong>{choice === item.target ? 'You have the complete target.' : 'You chose the other condition.'}</strong>{choice !== item.target && <p>You chose {choice}, but {item.word} refers to {item.target}. {id === 'group-1' ? 'You selected the result rather than the complete sufficient idea.' : 'You selected the condition that needs the requirement rather than the required idea.'}</p>}<p>{item.why}</p>{choice === item.target && <button className="co-primary" onClick={() => setDiagram(true)}>Place as the {id === 'group-1' ? 'sufficient' : 'required'} side</button>}</div>}</div>{diagram && <Arrow left={item.left} right={item.right}/>}</div>;
468	}
469	function Flashcards({ id }: { id: string }) {
470	  const cards = flashcardExamples[id];
471	  const [index, setIndex] = useState(0);
472	  const [back, setBack] = useState(false);
473	  const faceId = useId();
474	  const card = cards[index];
475	  return <div className="co-flash"><p className="co-kicker">Recall before revealing · {index + 1} / {cards.length}</p>
476	    <div className={`co-flash-face ${back ? 'co-flipped' : ''}`}><strong>{card.sentence}</strong><span>Say the complete rule, then compare.</span><div id={faceId} aria-live="polite">{back && <><strong>{card.readback}</strong><p>{card.why}</p></>}</div></div>
477	    <div className="co-choices"><button aria-expanded={back} aria-controls={faceId} onClick={() => setBack(!back)}>{back ? 'Hide answer' : 'Reveal answer'}</button><button disabled={index === 0} onClick={() => { setIndex(index - 1); setBack(false); }}>Previous sentence</button><button onClick={() => { setIndex((index + 1) % cards.length); setBack(false); }}>{index === cards.length - 1 ? 'Start again' : 'Next sentence'}</button></div><p className="co-small">These examples help you recall the relationship. Your attached drill gives you separate questions.</p></div>;
478	}
479	function DirectionSwitch() {
480	  const [only, setOnly] = useState(false);
481	  return <div className="co-switch"><div className="co-choices"><button aria-pressed={!only} onClick={() => setOnly(false)}>if</button><button aria-pressed={only} onClick={() => setOnly(true)}>only if</button></div><blockquote>A student enters <mark>{only ? 'only if' : 'if'}</mark> the student has a pass.</blockquote><Arrow left={only ? 'Enters' : 'Has pass'} right={only ? 'Has pass' : 'Enters'}/><p role="status">{only ? 'The pass is required. Having one does not establish entry.' : 'This sentence guarantees entry for a student who has a pass.'}</p></div>;
482	}
483	function DiagramBuilder({ id }: { id: string }) {
484	  const items = diagramExamples[id];
485	  const [index, setIndex] = useState(0);
486	  const [left, setLeft] = useState<string | null>(null);
487	  const [checked, setChecked] = useState(false);
488	  const item = items[index];
489	  return <div className="co-lab"><p className="co-kicker">Build the diagram</p><TargetSentence item={item} marked={checked && left === item.left}/><p>Both complete ideas are below. Choose the sufficient condition, the one that gives you enough to establish the other. It belongs on the left of the arrow.</p><div className="co-choices">{orderedConditionalIdeas(item).map(value => <button key={value} aria-pressed={left === value} onClick={() => { setLeft(value); setChecked(false); }}>{value}</button>)}</div>{left && <><Arrow left={left} right={left === item.target ? item.other : item.target}/><button className="co-primary" onClick={() => setChecked(true)}>Check my diagram</button></>}<div role="status">{checked && <div className={`co-feedback ${left === item.left ? 'co-correct' : ''}`}><strong>{left === item.left ? 'Your arrow preserves the relationship.' : 'Your arrow reverses this relationship.'}</strong>{left !== item.left && <p>{item.reversedPromise}</p>}<p>{item.why}</p><p>{item.readback}</p><button onClick={() => { setIndex((index + 1) % items.length); setLeft(null); setChecked(false); }}>{index === items.length - 1 ? 'Practice these again' : 'Next sentence'}</button></div>}</div></div>;
490	}
491	type IndicatorExplanation = {
492	  title: string;
493	  paragraphs: string[];
494	  prompt: string;
495	  reasoning: string[];
496	  after?: string[];
497	  check?: { prompt: string; options: string[]; correct: number; feedback: string[] };
498	};
499	
500	const indicatorExplanations: Record<string, IndicatorExplanation[]> = {
501	  'group-1': [
502	    {
503	      title: 'If: the starting condition can come last',
504	      paragraphs: [
505	        "Read the if-clause first: the report is submitted before noon. The sentence makes that enough for review. Every report meeting that condition is reviewed.",
506	        "The target of if is the complete idea submitted before noon. It appears second in the sentence, but it still has the sufficient role. Moving a clause doesn't change what guarantees what.",
507	      ],
508	      prompt: 'Which fact lets you establish review: submission before noon, or review itself?',
509	      reasoning: [
510	        "Submission before noon. That's the condition the rule makes enough for review. Start there and the sentence gives you the other condition.",
511	        "Starting from review instead would make a new promise about submission time. The original sentence hasn't told us that every reviewed report arrived before noon.",
512	      ],
513	      after: [
514	        "If can do another job. In The archivist asked if the drawer was locked, the sentence reports a question. You can read asked if as asked whether. It doesn't say a locked drawer guarantees an inquiry, or that an inquiry proves the drawer is locked.",
515	        "We've been told the archivist asked the question. We haven't been given its answer. There is no conditional rule to draw here, so read what the whole sentence says before treating if as a direction instruction.",
516	      ],
517	      check: {
518	        prompt: 'The archivist asked if the drawer was locked. What has the sentence established?',
519	        options: ['A locked drawer guarantees an inquiry.', 'The drawer was locked.', 'The archivist asked whether the drawer was locked.'],
520	        correct: 2,
521	        feedback: [
522	          'You changed a report of a question into a rule about locked drawers. Asked if tells us what the archivist asked, not what every locked drawer guarantees.',
523	          "You treated the question as if it supplied its own answer. The sentence establishes that the archivist asked whether the drawer was locked; it doesn't tell us that it was locked.",
524	          'This preserves the inquiry. Asked if means asked whether here. The sentence tells us a question was asked while leaving its answer open.',
525	        ],
526	      },
527	    },
528	    {
529	      title: 'When: an occasion can supply enough information',
530	      paragraphs: [
531	        "In this stated rule, the alarm sounding is enough for the staff to evacuate. When refers to that complete event. We're taking the sentence as an exceptionless relationship, rather than predicting what staff usually do.",
532	        "The staff evacuate when the alarm sounds expresses the same connection. The starting condition can appear after the result without changing its job.",
533	        "Whenever can express the same recurring relationship: Whenever the alarm sounds, the staff evacuate. Each alarm gives us evacuation in that sentence. It still doesn't tell us that every evacuation follows an alarm.",
534	      ],
535	      prompt: 'The staff are evacuating. Does this rule establish that the alarm sounded?',
536	      reasoning: [
537	        "No. The sentence tells us what follows when the alarm sounds. It hasn't said that an alarm is the only possible reason for evacuation.",
538	        "Staff evacuating for another reason would fit the rule. The alarm gives us evacuation; evacuation alone doesn't settle the alarm question.",

715	function IndicatorSections({ id }: { id: string }) {
716	  return <>{indicatorExplanations[id].map((lesson, index) => {
717	    const item = translationExamples[id][index];
718	    return <Section key={item.word} title={lesson.title} expressionNumber={index + 1}>
719	      <blockquote>{item.sentence}</blockquote>
720	      {lesson.paragraphs.map(paragraph => <p key={paragraph}>{paragraph}</p>)}
721	      {id === 'group-2' && item.word === 'only if' && <>
722	        <p>Try reading the sentence as though it concerns you. Imagine that you graduate and ask what you now have to know about your project. Then try starting from project completion and see whether the sentence settles graduation.</p>
723	        <p>This is a <strong>Personalized If/Then</strong>. Put yourself in the proposed starting condition, then check what the original sentence requires you to know. We're checking its meaning, not predicting what usually happens to graduates.</p>
724	        <ThinkPause prompt="If I graduate, what must be true of my project?">
725	          <p>I've completed it. The sentence requires project completion for anyone who graduates, including me in this imagined case.</p>
726	          <p>Graduates → Completes project matches that reading. Now try the reverse below.</p>
727	        </ThinkPause>
728	      </>}
729	      <ThinkPause prompt={lesson.prompt}>
730	        {lesson.reasoning.map(paragraph => <p key={paragraph}>{paragraph}</p>)}
731	        <Arrow left={item.left} right={item.right}/>
732	        <p className="co-readback">{item.readback}</p>
733	      </ThinkPause>
734	      {lesson.after?.map(paragraph => <p key={paragraph}>{paragraph}</p>)}
735	      {lesson.check && <Check {...lesson.check}/>}
736	      {id === 'group-2' && item.word === 'only if' && <DirectionSwitch/>}
737	    </Section>;
738	  })}</>;
739	}
