741	function Fundamentals({ roles = false }: { roles?: boolean }) {
742	  return <>
743	    {!roles && <>
744	      <Section title="One condition tells you something else">
745	        <p>A <strong>condition</strong> is something that can be true or false of the situation we're considering. A door is open. A person has a ticket. An order has been packed. Each gives us an idea whose truth we can ask about.</p>
746	        <p>A <strong>conditional statement</strong> connects two conditions. It tells us that if one is true, the other has to be true too. It doesn't tell us that the first condition actually occurred. It tells us what would follow if it did.</p>
747	        <p>That distinction matters. You can understand a rule about people who have tickets without knowing whether anyone has a ticket. Later, a fact about one person may give you a way to use the rule.</p>
748	        <p>You don't need symbols yet. First, understand what the sentence is saying.</p>
749	      </Section>
750	      <Section title="Why learn this?" dark>
751	        <p>On the LSAT, you're often deciding whether a conclusion follows from what you've been told. A conditional may supply the connection you need. It can also show you exactly where an argument asks you to accept more than its information supports.</p>
752	        <p>The important part is the direction. If one condition guarantees another, can we start with the other condition and go back? Another statement might allow that. But this rule alone doesn't.</p>
753	        <p>We'll work with one everyday rule and change what we know about a particular order. You'll see a conclusion the rule supports and another that would need more information. That distinction helps you check what must be true and decide whether an argument has gone too far.</p>
754	      </Section>
755	      <Section title="A guarantee is stronger than a tendency">
756	        <p>Compare these two statements:</p>
757	        <div className="co-intro-relationship"><div><span>A tendency</span><strong>Orders marked ready are usually packed.</strong><p>A ready order could be an exception.</p></div><div><span>A conditional rule</span><strong>Every order marked ready has been packed.</strong><p>A ready order cannot be an exception under this rule.</p></div></div>
758	        <p>Usually leaves room for exceptions. You could find an order marked ready that hasn't been packed and still accept the first statement. The second doesn't leave that possibility open. Every order with that label has been packed.</p>
759	        <p>For an LSAT question, take the stated rule as given. A real shop could make a mistake, but we're asking what follows from this particular statement. The rule gives us a guarantee within the information we're considering.</p>
760	        <p>Keep the sentence's own strength. A rule can give you permission to do something without saying you did it. It can also tell you what you're supposed to do without reporting that you followed the instruction. Those are different claims.</p>
761	        <blockquote>Every guest with a ticket may use the cloakroom.</blockquote>
762	        <p>May use gives permission here. A guest with a ticket is allowed to use the cloakroom. That guest could leave their coat on and never go inside. The permission is still there.</p>
763	        <ThinkPause prompt="Lena has a ticket. Do we know that she used the cloakroom?">
764	          <p>No. We know she's allowed to use it because the rule gives that permission to every guest with a ticket. We haven't been told whether she used it.</p>
765	          <p>Compare this instruction: <strong>If your coat is wet, hang it on the rack.</strong> A wet coat makes the instruction call for hanging it up. That doesn't report anyone carrying out the instruction. We'd need a fact about what they actually did.</p>
766	        </ThinkPause>
767	        <Check prompt="The notice says: If a tray is empty, return it to the counter. This tray is empty. What does the instruction establish?" options={['The tray has been returned.', 'The instruction calls for returning the tray.', 'The tray cannot be returned.']} correct={1} feedback={[
768	          "You turned an instruction into a completed action. The notice tells us what to do with an empty tray; it hasn't reported that anyone did it.",
769	          "The empty tray meets the condition in the instruction, so the notice calls for returning it. Whether someone has returned it is a separate question.",
770	          "The notice gives an instruction to return the tray. It doesn't say returning it is impossible. Keep what the sentence directs separate from what has actually happened.",
771	        ]}/>
772	        <p>Before drawing a conclusion, ask what the rule gives you: a fact, permission, or an instruction. Keep that meaning in your answer. Allowed to use isn't used, and told to return isn't returned.</p>
773	      </Section>
774	      <Section title="Read one rule in ordinary English">
775	        <blockquote>Every order marked ready has been packed.</blockquote>
776	        <p>There are two complete ideas here: an order is <strong>marked ready</strong>, and an order <strong>has been packed</strong>. The sentence tells us something about every order that meets the first condition.</p>
777	        <p>Read it this way: <strong>if an order is marked ready, then it has been packed.</strong> We don't need to know why the shop uses that label. We need to know what the sentence tells us about an order that has it.</p>
778	        <p>The rule itself doesn't identify a particular ready order. A separate fact can tell us whether one of the conditions is met in a particular case.</p>
779	        <ThinkPause prompt="Order A is marked ready. What do we now know about it?">
780	          <p><strong>Order A has been packed.</strong> The rule covers every order marked ready, and the fact tells us that Order A is one of those orders. That's the connection. The rule tells us what has to be true of the group; the fact lets us apply it to this case.</p>
781	          <p>We haven't learned that every order in the shop is packed. Our conclusion concerns Order A because that's the order the fact tells us about.</p>
782	        </ThinkPause>
783	      </Section>
784	      <Section title="The arrow is a shorter way to write it">
785	        <p>Now that we understand the sentence, we can give the two conditions shorter labels. The arrow records the connection between them.</p>
786	        <Arrow left="Marked ready" right="Packed"/>
787	        <p>Read it from left to right: <strong>if the order is marked ready, then it has been packed.</strong> Knowing the left condition lets us establish the right because every ready order is packed.</p>
788	        <p>The arrow doesn't mean “and then.” It also doesn't claim the label causes the packing. We're recording what follows logically, not a timeline.</p>
789	      </Section>
790	      <Section title="Change the fact, keep the rule">
791	        <p>Keep the rule exactly the same. This time, the fact tells us about packing instead of the ready label.</p>
792	        <blockquote>Every order marked ready has been packed.</blockquote>
793	        <ThinkPause prompt="Order B has been packed. Do we know whether it is marked ready?">
794	          <p><strong>We don't know.</strong> Order B could be marked ready. It could also be packed but not marked ready. Both possibilities fit the statement.</p>
795	          <p>The rule tells us about every ready order. It hasn't told us that every packed order gets that label. To conclude that Order B is ready, we'd need that additional connection.</p>
796	        </ThinkPause>
797	        <p>In the drawing below, ready orders sit inside packed orders because every ready order is packed. A packed order can fit inside or outside the ready group. Use the two facts to compare what each one establishes.</p>
798	        <SetExplorer/>
799	      </Section>
800	      <Section title="Try the same reasoning with a new rule">
801	        <p>You've used the rule in its stated direction and checked what happens when you start from its result. Try that distinction with a different sentence.</p>
802	        <Check prompt="Every volunteer wears a badge. Ari wears a badge. Is Ari necessarily a volunteer?" options={['Yes, the badge proves it.', 'No, Ari could have a badge for another reason.']} correct={1} feedback={['You used the rule backward. Every volunteer has a badge, but the sentence does not say everyone with a badge is a volunteer. A staff member wearing one would fit the rule too.', 'A badge alone leaves volunteer status unknown. The sentence allows a staff member with a badge; it has not restricted badges to volunteers.']}/>
803	        <p>A matching result can make the reverse feel plausible. Check whether the sentence actually gave you that direction before accepting it.</p>
804	        <p>We can now name the two jobs the conditions are doing. One is enough to establish the other. The other is required whenever the first holds.</p>
805	      </Section>
806	    </>}
807	    {roles && <>
808	      <Section title="Sufficient means enough">
809	        <p>An order being marked ready is sufficient for it being packed. It's enough. Once we know the order is marked ready, this rule gives us what we need to conclude it has been packed.</p>
810	        <blockquote>Every order marked ready has been packed.</blockquote>
811	        <p>You don't need a second fact about its contents. The rule already tells you what must be true of every order with that label. We call being marked ready the <strong>sufficient condition</strong>. Here, sufficient means enough to establish packing under this particular rule.</p>
812	        <ThinkPause prompt="Order C is marked ready. Do you need another fact about C to know it has been packed?">
813	          <p>No. The ready label meets the condition the rule tells us about. The rule and that fact are enough to establish packing.</p>
814	          <p>This doesn't make the label the only possible way to find out that an order is packed. It gives us one way to settle that question.</p>
815	        </ThinkPause>
816	      </Section>
817	      <Section title="Enough does not mean the only way">
818	        <p>Suppose Order D isn't marked ready. We no longer have the fact that allowed us to use the rule in its stated direction. But that doesn't settle the packing question.</p>
819	        <ThinkPause prompt="Order D isn't marked ready. Could it still have been packed?">
820	          <p>Yes. D could be packed without a ready label. It could also be unpacked. Neither possibility creates a ready order that hasn't been packed, so both fit the rule.</p>
821	          <p>The sentence gives us one condition that settles packing. It hasn't said packing happens only for those orders. When that sufficient condition isn't present, the other condition may still be true.</p>
822	        </ThinkPause>
823	        <p>Use a new rule to check whether you can recognize what is enough.</p>
824	        <Check prompt="Every delivery marked collected has a pickup receipt. What is sufficient here?" options={['Marked collected', 'Has a pickup receipt']} correct={0} feedback={['Marked collected is enough to establish a pickup receipt. The rule tells us what is true of every delivery with that label.', 'You chose the required result. The sentence gives a receipt to every delivery marked collected; it does not say every delivery with a receipt has that label.']}/>
825	      </Section>
826	      <Section title="Necessary means required">
827	        <p>Now look at the other condition in the same rule. An order marked ready has to have been packed. Being packed is necessary for being marked ready.</p>
828	        <p>We call being packed the <strong>necessary condition</strong>. Necessary means required. It names something that has to be true whenever the sufficient condition is true.</p>
829	        <p>Ask “required for what?” Here, required for an order marked ready. The relationship gives the word its meaning.</p>
830	        <ThinkPause prompt="Which condition does a ready order have to meet?">
831	          <p>It has to be packed. Otherwise we'd have a ready order that wasn't packed, which the sentence rules out.</p>
832	          <p>The ready label is enough to establish packing, and packing is required for every ready order. Those are two views of the same relationship.</p>
833	        </ThinkPause>
834	        <div className="co-role-pair"><div><h3>Sufficient</h3><p>Marked ready: enough to establish packing.</p><strong>Goes left</strong></div><div><h3>Necessary</h3><p>Packed: required for every ready order.</p><strong>Goes right</strong></div></div>
835	        <p>Now compare what each starting fact tells us. Keep the same rule while you switch between the ready label and packing.</p>
836	        <GateExplorer/>
837	      </Section>
838	      <Section title="Having a requirement does not guarantee the result">
839	        <p>Let's use a different requirement.</p>
840	        <blockquote>Entering the archive requires a pass.</blockquote>
841	        <p>The sentence tells us what someone needs for entry: a pass. If someone enters the archive, they have that pass. That's why has a pass belongs on the required side.</p>
842	        <Arrow left="Enters the archive" right="Has a pass"/>
843	        <ThinkPause prompt="Mina has a pass. Does this sentence establish that she entered the archive?">
844	          <p>No. Mina could have a pass and remain outside. The rule tells us what entry requires; it doesn't tell us that every person with a pass enters.</p>
845	          <p>Necessary doesn't mean enough. A requirement can be present while the other condition remains unknown. To conclude Mina entered, we'd need information establishing that additional connection.</p>
846	        </ThinkPause>
847	        <Check prompt="Every finalist receives an invitation. Which requirement does this sentence establish?" options={['Receiving an invitation is necessary for being a finalist.', 'Being a finalist is necessary for receiving an invitation.']} correct={0} feedback={['The sentence gives an invitation to every finalist. That makes receiving one required for finalist status; it does not restrict invitations to finalists.', "You reversed the requirement. Your choice would mean anyone receiving an invitation has to be a finalist. The sentence allows someone who isn't a finalist to receive an invitation too."]}/>
848	      </Section>
849	      <Section title="Try the sentence from the inside">
850	        <p>We can check a proposed arrow by imagining a case in which its left side is true. Read the original sentence and ask what you now have to know about that same case. Your answer should match the right side of the arrow.</p>
851	        <p>This is a <strong>Personalized If/Then</strong>. Read the rule as if it concerns you or something of yours. The point is to test what the sentence guarantees, not guess what usually happens.</p>
852	        <blockquote>Every order marked ready has been packed.</blockquote>
853	        <p>For this rule, imagine an order of yours. Keep the same order in mind on both sides.</p>
854	        <ThinkPause prompt="If my order is marked ready, what do I know about it?">
855	          <p>It has been packed. Every order marked ready is packed, and my imagined order has that label. Marked ready → Packed matches what the sentence tells me.</p>
856	        </ThinkPause>
857	        <ThinkPause prompt="If my order has been packed, must it be marked ready?">
858	          <p>No. My order could be packed without a ready label. That fits every ready order is packed because it doesn't create a ready order that hasn't been packed.</p>
859	          <p>Packed → Marked ready would add a claim about every packed order. The sentence doesn't give me that direction. Trying both readings lets me catch the reverse instead of accepting an arrow just because it looks familiar.</p>
860	        </ThinkPause>
861	      </Section>
862	      <Section title="Read the roles in the relationship">
863	        <p>In <strong>Marked ready → Packed</strong>, the left condition is sufficient for the right: it's enough to establish it. The right condition is necessary for the left: it's required whenever the left holds.</p>
864	        <p>These names describe roles in this rule. A different sentence could give the same idea a different role. Don't ask whether a pass is sufficient or necessary by itself. Ask what the complete sentence says having the pass does.</p>
865	        <p>The next two groups give us ways to recognize these roles in varied wording. We'll keep returning to the meaning because an indicator can only help if you know what complete idea it refers to.</p>
866	      </Section>
867	    </>}
868	  </>;
869	}

871	function GroupLessons({ id }: { id: string }) {
872	  const group = GROUPS.find(item => item.id === (id === 'group-1' ? 'g1' : 'g2'))!;
873	  const first = id === 'group-1';
874	  return <>
875	    <Section title={first ? 'Find the sufficient idea' : 'Find what is required'}>
876	      <p>{first
877	        ? "A sufficient condition gives you enough information to establish the other condition. Group 1 helps you recognize that job in several kinds of sentence. We'll work through the meaning of each expression, then practice finding its complete target."
878	        : "A necessary condition is something the other condition requires. Group 2 helps us recognize what must be true when that other condition holds. The requirement belongs on the right because the left condition cannot hold without it. Having the requirement, though, leaves the left condition unknown unless something else settles it."}</p>
879	      <div className="co-vocabulary">{group.words.map(word => <span key={word}>{word}</span>)}</div>
880	      <p>An <strong>indicator</strong> is an expression that helps us recognize the relationship. Its <strong>target</strong> is the complete idea it refers to. Finding that idea matters more than taking the next few words in the sentence.</p>
881	    </Section>
882	    <IndicatorSections id={id}/>
883	    <Section title={first ? 'Find the target, then place it' : 'Find the requirement, then place it'}>
884	      <p>{first
885	        ? "Add what to the indicator when that gives you a sensible question. If what? All what? The only what? Your answer should be the whole idea the expression refers to. Then check whether that idea really is enough to establish the other condition."
886	        : "Ask what the indicator refers to. Only what? Only if what? What is required? The target can come before or after the expression, so check its meaning in the complete sentence."}</p>
887	      <p>This is the <strong>What Test</strong>. The question helps you find the indicator's complete target before choosing its side of the arrow. Keep any words that limit which people, objects, places, or occasions the sentence covers. The question finds the idea; the whole sentence tells you what that idea does.</p>
888	      <p>Work through the expressions below. First choose the complete target. Then place it on its side of the arrow and read the relationship in ordinary English.</p>
889	      <TranslationLab id={id}/>
890	    </Section>
891	    <Section title="Keep the whole condition" dark>
892	      <blockquote>{first ? 'Any applicant who submits late pays a fee.' : 'Entry requires a current pass.'}</blockquote>
893	      <p>{first
894	        ? "Applicant alone isn't the condition. The sentence is about an applicant who submits late. Keep that restriction when you shorten the idea; otherwise your diagram would make a claim about on-time applicants too."
895	        : "A current pass is required. Has a pass drops current and would include an expired pass. Shortening a label should save writing while preserving what the sentence requires."}</p>
896	      <Check prompt={first ? 'What goes left in this rule?' : 'What goes right in this rule?'} options={first ? ['Applicant', 'Applicant submits late'] : ['Has current pass', 'Has any pass']} correct={first ? 1 : 0} feedback={first
897	        ? ['You dropped the late-submission restriction. Applicant alone would cover every applicant, including someone who submits on time. The sufficient idea is applicant submits late.', 'This keeps the complete sufficient idea. The rule covers applicants who submit late, so keep that restriction with the applicant.']
898	        : ['This keeps the full requirement. Entry needs a current pass; an expired pass does not meet that requirement.', 'You dropped current from the requirement. Has any pass would include an expired pass, which is not what the sentence requires.']}/>
899	    </Section>
900	    <Section title={first ? 'Read the only as a complete expression' : 'Compare only with the only'}>
901	      {first ? <>
902	        <blockquote>The only people admitted are members.</blockquote>
903	        <p>The only what? The only people admitted. The complete target is admitted people. If someone is admitted, the sentence tells us they're a member.</p>
904	        <Arrow left="Admitted" right="Member"/>
905	        <p>A member who stays outside fits this rule. Nothing says every member is admitted. Check that your diagram keeps that possibility open.</p>
906	        <Check prompt="The only vehicles permitted inside are electric. Which diagram preserves the rule?" options={['Electric → Permitted inside', 'Permitted inside → Electric']} correct={1} feedback={['You made electric status enough for permission. The sentence requires permitted vehicles to be electric; it does not promise admission to every electric vehicle.', 'Permitted vehicles have to be electric. This direction preserves the limit on permitted vehicles without adding a promise about all electric vehicles.']}/>
907	      </> : <>
908	        <p>Try the distinction on the order rule we already understand. The wording changes while the connection stays the same.</p>
909	        <div className="co-word-comparison"><div><blockquote>Only packed orders are marked ready.</blockquote><p>Only refers to packed orders. Packing is required for the ready label, so packed goes right.</p></div><div><blockquote>The only orders marked ready are packed orders.</blockquote><p>The only refers to orders marked ready. That label is enough to establish packing, so marked ready goes left.</p></div></div>
910	        <Arrow left="Marked ready" right="Packed"/>
911	        <p>Both sentences preserve the same direction. An order that is packed but not marked ready fits both. Neither sentence makes packing alone enough for the label.</p>
912	        <Check prompt="Only registered guests receive wristbands. What follows from receiving a wristband?" options={['The person is a registered guest.', 'Nothing about registration.']} correct={0} feedback={['Only limits wristbands to registered guests, so receiving one establishes that the person is registered.', 'You treated the sentence like all registered guests receive wristbands. Only restricts who receives one, so the wristband does establish that the person is a registered guest.']}/>
913	      </>}
914	    </Section>
915	    <Section title="From sentence to diagram">
916	      <p>You have seen the expressions with help. Now use a new sentence. Identify both complete ideas, choose the sufficient one for the left, and check whether your diagram says what the sentence says.</p>
917	      <p>If you know the meaning but hesitate over the arrow, imagine the left condition is true. Does the sentence then require the right condition? Read the whole relationship in English. If it makes a promise the original didn't make, change the direction.</p>
918	      <DiagramBuilder id={id}/>
919	      <details className="co-practical-note"><summary>Why write a diagram when I understand the sentence?</summary><p>For one short sentence, you may be able to hold the connection in your head. Here, writing it makes your direction and complete conditions visible so you can check them. If your first diagram doesn't match the meaning, find the exact phrase you misread and revise that part.</p></details>
920	    </Section>
921	    <Section title="Say the rule without the scaffold">
922	      <p>Read the new sentence and explain its direction before revealing. If you hesitate, ask what the indicator refers to, then try the proposed relationship in ordinary English. Compare your explanation with the one that appears.</p>
923	      <Flashcards id={id}/>
924	    </Section>
925	    <Section title="Check the meaning, not just the word">
926	      <Check prompt={first ? 'A report is reviewed if submitted before noon. Which is the same rule?' : 'A student graduates only if the project is completed. Which is the same rule?'} options={first ? ['If reviewed, it was submitted before noon.', 'If submitted before noon, it is reviewed.'] : ['If the student graduates, the project was completed.', 'If the project is completed, the student graduates.']} correct={first ? 1 : 0} feedback={first
927	        ? ['You reversed the if relationship. Submission before noon is enough for review; the sentence has not said review proves when the report was submitted.', 'This preserves the if-clause as the sufficient condition. Submission before noon gives us review even though it appears second in the original sentence.']
928	        : ['This preserves project completion as a requirement for graduation. It does not turn completing the project into a promise of graduation.', 'You turned the only-if requirement into a guarantee. Graduation requires completion, but the sentence allows a completed project without graduation.']}/>
929	      <p>The guided practice asks you to write your reasoning with the method beside you. Then the attached drill gives you fresh questions. Use the explanation to inspect the direction and complete conditions, not only whether you chose the same answer.</p>
930	    </Section>
931	  </>;
932	}

934	function OpeningFigure({ unitId }: { unitId: string }) {
935	  if (unitId === 'orientation') return <figure className="co-hero-figure co-meaning-figure">
936	    <p className="co-kicker">A connection between conditions</p>
937	    <div className="co-meaning-premise"><span>One condition</span><strong>If this is true...</strong></div>
938	    <div className="co-meaning-result"><span>Another condition</span><strong>...this has to be true too.</strong></div>
939	    <figcaption>The statement connects them. It doesn't say either one has occurred.</figcaption>
940	  </figure>;
941	  if (unitId === 'sufficient-necessary') return <figure className="co-hero-figure co-roles-figure">
942	    <p className="co-kicker">Two views of one rule</p>
943	    <p className="co-hero-rule">Every order marked ready has been packed.</p>
944	    <div className="co-role-compass"><div><span>Enough</span><strong>Marked ready</strong><small>Gives us packing</small></div><div><span>Required</span><strong>Packed</strong><small>Every ready order needs it</small></div></div>
945	    <figcaption>One condition is enough. The other is required by that same rule.</figcaption>
946	  </figure>;
947	  if (unitId === 'group-1') return <figure className="co-hero-figure co-sufficient-figure">
948	    <p className="co-kicker">Read the complete idea</p>
949	    <p className="co-hero-sentence">A report is reviewed <mark>if</mark> <strong>it is submitted before noon.</strong></p>
950	    <div className="co-hero-target"><span>What is enough for review?</span><strong>Submission before noon</strong></div>
951	    <figcaption>The sufficient condition can come last in the sentence.</figcaption>
952	  </figure>;
953	  return <figure className="co-hero-figure co-required-figure">
954	    <p className="co-kicker">Read what the sentence needs</p>
955	    <p className="co-hero-sentence">Entry requires a <strong>current pass.</strong></p>
956	    <div className="co-pass-figure"><span>Required for entry</span><strong>A current pass</strong><small>An expired pass won't meet this requirement.</small></div>
957	    <figcaption>Having the pass alone doesn't establish entry.</figcaption>
958	  </figure>;
959	}

961	export function ConditionalOpeningLesson({ unit, onComplete, continueHref, continueLabel = 'Continue to guided practice', previousHref, reviewed }: ConditionalLessonNoteProps) {
962	  const [motionPaused, setMotionPaused] = useState(false);
963	  const [activeInteraction, setActiveInteraction] = useState<HTMLElement | null>(null);
964	  const [tocHidden, setTocHidden] = useState(false);
965	  useEffect(() => {
966	    if (!activeInteraction) { setTocHidden(false); return; }
967	    setTocHidden(true);
968	    if (typeof IntersectionObserver === 'undefined') return;
969	    const observer = new IntersectionObserver(([entry]) => setTocHidden(entry.isIntersecting));
970	    observer.observe(activeInteraction);
971	    return () => observer.disconnect();
972	  }, [activeInteraction]);
973	  const protectInteraction = (target: EventTarget) => {
974	    if (!(target instanceof HTMLElement)) return;
975	    const interaction = target.closest<HTMLElement>('.co-lab,.co-think-pause,.co-flash,.co-check');
976	    if (interaction) { setTocHidden(true); setActiveInteraction(interaction); }
977	    else if (target.closest('button,a')) setActiveInteraction(null);
978	  };
979	  const group = unit.id === 'group-1' || unit.id === 'group-2';
980	  const orientation = unit.id === 'orientation';
981	  const title = group ? unit.id === 'group-1' ? 'Group 1: what is enough?' : 'Group 2: what is required?' : orientation ? 'What Are Conditionals?' : 'What is enough? What is required?';
982	  const repairTitle = orientation ? "Don't turn a rule into a case fact" : unit.id === 'sufficient-necessary' ? "Don't turn a requirement into a guarantee" : unit.id === 'group-1' ? 'Keep the direction and the whole condition' : 'Keep requirements and completed actions distinct';
983	  const subtitle = orientation
984	    ? "A conditional tells us that if one thing is true, something else has to be true too. Let's see what that connection lets you conclude, and what it leaves open."
985	    : group ? unit.id === 'group-1'
986	      ? 'These expressions can point to the sufficient idea. Read what each one says before deciding which complete condition belongs on the left.'
987	      : 'These expressions can identify a requirement. Find the complete idea the sentence says you need, then check what its presence does and does not prove.'
988	    : "The same rule gives its two conditions different jobs. Let's take each one seriously before giving it a place in the diagram.";
989	  return <div className="conditional-note co-opening" data-motion-paused={motionPaused} onFocusCapture={event => protectInteraction(event.target)} onClickCapture={event => protectInteraction(event.target)}>
990	    <div className="co-lesson-navigation" hidden={tocHidden}><DynamicIslandTOC key={unit.id} selector=".co-opening [data-toc]"/></div>
991	    <header className={'co-hero ' + (orientation ? 'co-intro-hero' : '')}>
992	      <div className="co-hero-copy"><p className="co-kicker">Conditional logic</p><h1>{title}</h1><p>{subtitle}</p>
993	      <a href="#co-reading">{orientation ? 'Start with the meaning' : 'Begin reading'} <span aria-hidden="true">↓</span></a></div>
994	      <OpeningFigure unitId={unit.id}/>
995	    </header>
996	    <article id="co-reading">
997	      {group ? <GroupLessons id={unit.id}/> : <Fundamentals roles={unit.id === 'sufficient-necessary'}/>}
998	      <ConditionalOpeningMedia unitId={unit.id}/>
999	      <Section id="repair" title={repairTitle}>
1000	        <div className="co-word-comparison"><div><h3>The tempting move</h3><p>{unit.commonMistake.mistake}</p></div><div><h3>Read the sentence again</h3><p>{unit.commonMistake.repair}</p></div></div>
1001	      </Section>
1002	      <footer className="co-footer">
1003	        <button type="button" className="co-motion-toggle" aria-pressed={motionPaused} onClick={() => setMotionPaused(!motionPaused)}>Reduce animation in this lesson</button>
1004	        <p>{reviewed ? 'You marked this lesson as read. You can return to the examples whenever you need.' : 'Marking this lesson as read records review. Your practice gives you a chance to use the reasoning yourself.'}</p>
1005	        <div className="co-choices">{previousHref && <Link to={previousHref}>Previous lesson</Link>}<button type="button" onClick={onComplete}>{reviewed ? 'Lesson read' : 'Mark lesson as read'}</button><Link className="co-primary" to={continueHref}>{continueLabel} <span aria-hidden="true">→</span></Link></div>
1006	      </footer>
1007	    </article>
1008	  </div>;
1009	}

180	function Lesson() {

184	  const unit = findConditionalUnit(unitId ?? '');

193	  return <ConditionalLessonNote unit={unit} reviewed={!!progress.lessons[unit.id] && !needsExpandedConditionalLessonReview(progress.lessons, unit.id)} onComplete={() => update(prior => markConditionalLessonRead(prior, unit.id))} continueHref={actionUrl(unit, 'guided', target)} previousHref={previous ? actionUrl(previous, 'lesson', target) : undefined} />;

28	    id: 'orientation', title: "What Are Conditionals?", area: 'understanding', phase: 'conditional', prerequisites: [],
29	    goal: "Explain what a conditional says, use it with a fact, and say what the information leaves open.",
30	    teaching: [
31	      "A conditional tells us that if one condition is true, another has to be true too. A condition is something we can say about a situation, such as an order being marked ready or its having been packed. The conditional connects those two ideas. It doesn't tell us that either one has happened.",
32	      "Why learn this? A conclusion on the LSAT can depend on which way that connection runs. If you know what the rule promises, you can explain why a conclusion follows. If an answer uses the rule in the other direction, you can see the missing connection instead of accepting a conclusion that sounds reasonable.",
33	      "Let's use one rule: Every order marked ready has been packed. Take that claim as true for this exercise. Every ready order has to be packed. Orders marked ready are usually packed would say something weaker, because usually leaves room for an exception. Our rule doesn't.",
34	      "Now we get a fact: Order A is marked ready. What do you know about A? Take a moment to explain your answer before reading on. A has been packed. The rule covers every order marked ready, and the fact puts A in that group. A ready order that hadn't been packed would break the rule.",
35	      "Keep the rule and change the fact: Order B has been packed. Must B be marked ready? Try to explain why before reading on. We don't know. The rule told us about ready orders. It didn't say every packed order is marked ready. B could be marked ready or packed without being marked ready. Either possibility fits what we've been told.",
36	      "We can now shorten the relationship to Marked ready → Packed. Read that as: if an order is marked ready, it has been packed. The arrow records the connection we've already understood. It doesn't add a cause, a time sequence, or a fact about a particular order.",
37	      "In the practice, separate the rule from the fact about the case. Then ask what that fact lets you conclude. Write the reason as well as the answer. That gives you something specific to compare with the explanation if your choice goes wrong.",
38	    ],
39	    method: [
40	      "Read the rule in ordinary English: what does it say has to be true if the first condition is true?",
41	      "Check the strength. Every ready order is packed gives a guarantee; usually packed allows an exception.",
42	      "Find the separate fact about the case. A rule alone doesn't identify a ready order.",
43	      "Compare the whole fact with the condition the rule covers. Order A is marked ready meets our ready-order condition.",
44	      "State what follows and why. If the information leaves a question open, show how both possibilities could fit.",
45	    ],
46	    workedExample: {

308	it('keeps navigation away from active lab feedback and restores it when that region leaves view', () => {

312	    constructor(callback: typeof visibility) { visibility = callback; }
313	    observe = observe;
314	    disconnect = vi.fn();
315	  });
316	  const { container, complete } = mount('group-2');
317	  const navigation = container.querySelector<HTMLElement>('.co-lesson-navigation')!;
318	  const lab = screen.getByText('Read the whole idea').closest<HTMLElement>('.co-lab')!;

322	  expect(navigation.hidden).toBe(true);
323	  expect(observe).toHaveBeenCalledWith(lab);
324	  act(() => visibility!([{ isIntersecting: false }]));
325	  expect(navigation.hidden).toBe(false);
326	  act(() => visibility!([{ isIntersecting: true }]));
327	  expect(navigation.hidden).toBe(true);

136	function observeRepairScrolling(run: (scrolled: HTMLElement[]) => void) {
137	  const original = Object.getOwnPropertyDescriptor(HTMLElement.prototype, 'scrollIntoView');
138	  const scrolled: HTMLElement[] = [];
139	  Object.defineProperty(HTMLElement.prototype, 'scrollIntoView', { configurable: true, value: vi.fn(function (this: HTMLElement) { scrolled.push(this); }) });
140	  try { run(scrolled); }
141	  finally {
142	    if (original) Object.defineProperty(HTMLElement.prototype, 'scrollIntoView', original);
143	    else Reflect.deleteProperty(HTMLElement.prototype, 'scrollIntoView');
144	  }
145	}

26	    case 'capstone': return `${prefix} Capstone`;
27	    default: return 'Continue';
28	  }

1	import { useId, useState } from 'react';
2	
3	type Card = { question: string; answer: string };
4	// Reviewed derivatives of NotebookLM cards, 2026-10-02. See the vault asset
5	// manifest and REVIEW.md for source IDs, raw originals and corrections.
6	const cards: Record<string, Card[]> = {
7	  orientation: [
8	    { question: 'What does a conditional statement tell you?', answer: 'If one condition is true, the other has to be true too. The statement gives you that connection; it does not establish that either condition actually occurs. A matching fact can let you use the rule for a particular case.' },
9	    { question: 'Every locker marked reserved has a name card. Locker 12 is marked reserved. What do we know?', answer: 'Locker 12 has a name card. The rule covers every reserved locker, and the fact tells us Locker 12 belongs to that group. That matching condition is why the conclusion follows.' },
10	    { question: 'Every locker marked reserved has a name card. Locker 9 has a name card. Is it necessarily marked reserved?', answer: 'No. The sentence tells us what every reserved locker has. It has not restricted name cards to reserved lockers. Locker 9 could be reserved or unreserved; its name card alone leaves that question open.' },
11	    { question: 'Why do conditionals matter when evaluating an argument?', answer: 'They can supply the connection that makes a conclusion follow. They can also show where an author needs more information. If the argument uses a rule backward, check whether anything actually gave it that additional direction.' },
12	  ],
13	  'sufficient-necessary': [
14	    { question: 'What does sufficient mean?', answer: 'Enough to establish the other condition under this rule. Once the sufficient condition is met, the rule gives you the necessary condition. Enough does not mean it is the only possible way for the other condition to be true.' },
15	    { question: 'Every order marked ready has been packed. Does packing establish the ready label?', answer: "No. Every ready order has to be packed, but the sentence doesn't say every packed order is marked ready. A packed order without a ready label fits the rule. We need more information to settle its ready status." },
16	    { question: 'What does necessary mean?', answer: 'Required whenever the sufficient condition holds. Ask required for what, and read the complete relationship. In Marked ready → Packed, packing is required for every ready order; those roles belong to this particular rule.' },
17	    { question: 'If a requirement is met, does that prove the other condition happened?', answer: 'No. A requirement may be present while the other condition remains unknown. Entry requires a pass, but a person could have a pass and stay outside. The rule has not turned the pass into a guarantee of entry.' },
18	  ],
19	  'group-1': [
20	    { question: 'A student receives feedback if the student revises the essay. Write the rule.', answer: 'Revises essay → Receives feedback. If refers to revising, even though that clause appears last. The sentence gives feedback to each student who revises. Feedback alone does not establish that the student revised.' },
21	    { question: 'Any applicant who submits late pays a fee. Why is “Applicant → Pays fee” too broad?', answer: 'It drops the late-submission restriction. Applicant alone would include someone who submitted on time, whom the sentence does not cover. Keep the complete rule: Applicant submits late → Pays fee.' },
22	    { question: 'Every student who registers early receives a discount. Write the rule.', answer: 'Student registers early → Receives discount. Every refers to students who register early. Early registration is part of the sufficient condition, so keep it when you shorten the idea.' },
23	    { question: 'A fee is paid by any applicant who submits late. Write the rule.', answer: 'Applicant submits late → Pays fee. Any still refers to late-submitting applicants. Moving that phrase later does not change its sufficient role. Read the arrow as: if an applicant submits late, that applicant pays a fee.' },
24	  ],
25	  'group-2': [
26	    { question: 'A permit is required for construction. Write the rule.', answer: 'Construction → Has permit. What is required? The permit, even though it appears before required. The sentence makes it necessary for construction, not enough to establish that construction occurs.' },
27	    { question: 'Why not automatically take the words immediately after the indicator?', answer: 'The complete target can appear before or after the expression. In a permit is required for construction, the permit is the requirement. Ask what the indicator refers to, then check your diagram against the meaning of the whole sentence.' },
28	    { question: 'A finalist must attend the interview. Maya attended. Does that prove she is a finalist?', answer: 'No. The rule gives finalists an obligation; it does not say only finalists attend. Actual attendance is also different from being required to attend. The fact about Maya does not establish finalist status.' },
29	    { question: 'A garden light switches on only when its sensor detects darkness. Write the rule.', answer: 'Light switches on → Sensor detects darkness. Only when makes darkness detection required for switching on. The sentence has not said detection is enough, so knowing the sensor detects darkness alone would leave the light unknown.' },
30	  ],
31	};
32	
33	function Recall({ items }: { items: Card[] }) {
34	  const [index, setIndex] = useState(0);
35	  const [revealed, setRevealed] = useState(false);
36	  const answerId = useId();
37	  const item = items[index];
38	  return <div className="co-flash">
39	    <p className="co-kicker">Review · {index + 1} of {items.length}</p>
40	    <div className={`co-flash-face ${revealed ? 'co-flipped' : ''}`}>
41	      <strong>{item.question}</strong><span>Answer in your own words, then compare.</span>
42	      <div id={answerId} aria-live="polite">{revealed && <p>{item.answer}</p>}</div>
43	    </div>
44	    <div className="co-choices">
45	      <button type="button" aria-expanded={revealed} aria-controls={answerId} onClick={() => setRevealed(!revealed)}>{revealed ? 'Hide recall explanation' : 'Reveal recall explanation'}</button>
46	      <button type="button" disabled={index === 0} onClick={() => { setIndex(index - 1); setRevealed(false); }}>Previous example</button>
47	      <button type="button" onClick={() => { setIndex((index + 1) % items.length); setRevealed(false); }}>{index === items.length - 1 ? 'Start these again' : 'Next example'}</button>
48	    </div>
49	  </div>;
50	}
51	
52	export function ConditionalOpeningMedia({ unitId }: { unitId: string }) {
53	  // Group lessons already have seven original sentence-to-rule recall cards.
54	  // Their final meaning check closes the lesson without repeating a second deck.
55	  if (unitId === 'group-1' || unitId === 'group-2') return null;
56	  const items = cards[unitId];
57	  if (!items) return null;
58	  return <section id="co-return-to-the-relationship" className="co-section" data-toc data-toc-title="Return to the relationship">
59	    <h2>Return to the relationship</h2>
60	    <p>Try these before moving on. Say what the rule gives you and explain why.</p>
61	    <Recall key={unitId} items={items}/>
62	    {unitId === 'sufficient-necessary' && <details className="co-media-summary">
63	      <summary>See the relationships together</summary>
64	      <figure className="co-relationship-overview">
65	        <h3>Every order marked ready has been packed.</h3>
66	        <div className="co-overview-roles"><div><strong>Marked ready</strong><span>Sufficient: enough</span></div><b aria-hidden="true">→</b><span className="co-sr-only">implies</span><div><strong>Packed</strong><span>Necessary: required</span></div></div>
67	        <div className="co-overview-set">
68	          <svg viewBox="0 0 600 250" role="img" aria-labelledby="co-order-overview-title co-order-overview-desc">
69	            <title id="co-order-overview-title">Marked ready is sufficient. Packed is necessary.</title>
70	            <desc id="co-order-overview-desc">The ready group is contained within packed orders. All ready orders are packed. Packed alone does not establish the ready label. The drawing does not require unequal group sizes.</desc>
71	            <rect x="10" y="10" width="580" height="230" rx="24" fill="#d7e9df"/>
72	            <rect x="40" y="78" width="285" height="132" rx="18" fill="#fcf3df" stroke="#bd8a47"/>
73	          </svg>
74	          <span className="co-overview-packed-label">Packed orders</span><span className="co-overview-ready-label">Orders marked ready</span>
75	        </div>
76	        <p>Packed alone: the ready label remains unknown.</p>
77	        <figcaption>Read the arrow in its stated direction. An order could be packed without a ready label.</figcaption>
78	      </figure>
79	    </details>}
80	  </section>;
81	}
